## Conversation

### 🤖 Assistant
*2026-09-10 16:20:34*

refactr using this template, do not break functionality
Yes. Your criticism is correct. The previous version mixed **phases, artifacts, principles, and individual mechanisms** into one numbered list, which makes it impossible to see the actual operating loop.

The biggest correction is to separate the system into:

1. **Project initialization** — done once, establishes the durable repository context.
2. **Development loop** — repeated for every task/feature.
3. **Release loop** — turns accumulated work into a release.
4. **Evolution loop** — used when adding features or changing architecture.
5. **Failure/escalation rules** — rules that apply inside the development loop rather than being separate phases.

And yes: **MVP needs a persistent artifact.** Otherwise the decision made during the conversation disappears.

I would change your system substantially as follows.

# The actual system

```text
                     PROJECT INITIALIZATION
                              │
        ┌─────────────────────┼─────────────────────┐
        ↓                     ↓                     ↓
   PRODUCT.md             MVP.md              ARCHITECTURE.md
        │                     │                     │
        └─────────────────────┼─────────────────────┘
                              ↓
                    ENGINEERING BASELINE
                              │
             ┌────────────────┴────────────────┐
             ↓                                 ↓
       QUALITY / RULES                   IMPLEMENTATION
       + verification                       GRAPH
             │                                 │
             └────────────────┬────────────────┘
                              ↓
                    ┌─────────────────┐
                    │ DEVELOPMENT LOOP│
                    └─────────────────┘
                              │
                    repeat until MVP
                              │
                              ↓
                            RELEASE
                              │
                              ↓
                    ┌─────────────────┐
                    │ EVOLUTION LOOP  │
                    └─────────────────┘
                              │
                         repeat forever
```

That's the structure I'd build the whole methodology around.

---

# PART I — Project initialization

This starts when you have:

> "I have an idea for an app."

This phase happens once, although its artifacts can later be revised.

---

## 1. Product definition

### WHAT

You explore the product with a strong reasoning model.

The result is the durable product specification.

### WHO

You + reasoning model.

### ARTIFACT

```text
PRODUCT.md
```

It contains:

```text
Problem
Target users
Core user journeys
Functional requirements
UX requirements
Constraints
Non-goals
Important assumptions
Open questions
```

The important point is:

**PRODUCT.md is the authoritative source for what the product is supposed to do.**

Not the architecture documents.

Not the implementation plan.

Not the agent instructions.

### WHAT YOU DECIDE

You approve the product definition.

### OUTPUT

A durable product specification that survives the end of the conversation.

---

# 2. Define the MVP

This should absolutely produce a persistent file.

I would use:

```text
MVP.md
```

### WHY

Because:

```text
PRODUCT.md
```

answers:

> What is this product?

while:

```text
MVP.md
```

answers:

> What are we actually building **right now**?

That distinction is extremely useful.

### MVP.md contains

```text
MVP goal

Core user journey(s)

Included capabilities

Excluded capabilities

Acceptance criteria

Known limitations

Deferred features
```

For example:

```text
PRODUCT
├── import configs
├── edit configs
├── validate configs
├── export configs
├── multiple providers
├── cloud sync
├── collaboration
└── analytics

MVP
├── import configs
├── edit configs
├── validate configs
└── export configs

DEFERRED
├── cloud sync
├── collaboration
└── analytics
```

### WHO

You + reasoning model.

### IMPORTANT

You approve this.

An agent should **not independently decide the MVP scope while coding**.

### OUTPUT

A persistent statement of:

> This is the scope we are trying to make work first.

---

# 3. Technical architecture

Now we move from:

> What are we building?

to:

> How should the software be structured?

Here I agree with your criticism.

Do **not** force every project to contain:

```text
DATA_MODEL.md
API_CONTRACTS.md
TECHNICAL_DECISIONS.md
...
```

That's too rigid.

Instead:

### Mandatory

```text
ARCHITECTURE.md
```

### Optional documents

The architecture-designing model decides what additional artifacts are actually useful.

For example:

```text
ARCHITECTURE.md
DATA_MODEL.md
API_CONTRACTS.md
STATE_MODEL.md
SECURITY.md
PERFORMANCE.md
INTEGRATION.md
DOMAIN_X.md
...
```

A tiny CLI may need almost nothing.

A database-heavy backend may benefit greatly from `DATA_MODEL.md`.

A stateful Flutter application may need a `STATE_MODEL.md`.

An API-heavy application may need `API_CONTRACTS.md`.

A browser extension integrating Yomitan may need integration-specific documentation.

### This is the instruction to the architecture model

Not:

> Create these seven files.

Instead:

> Design the technical architecture for the MVP. Create `ARCHITECTURE.md` as the authoritative architectural document. Create additional technical documents only where they contain substantial information that would otherwise make `ARCHITECTURE.md` unwieldy or where a domain needs an independently maintained contract/specification.

That gives the senior model freedom.

---

# 4. Diagrams

Diagrams are **tools**, not mandatory deliverables.

The architecture model should create diagrams when they clarify something that prose doesn't.

Useful candidates:

### Architecture/component diagram

Useful almost always for anything beyond a tiny application.

Shows:

```text
UI
 ↓
Application layer
 ↓
Domain
 ↓
Repositories
 ↓
Persistence
```

### Data/entity diagram

Only when relationships between persistent entities matter.

### Sequence diagram

Useful for complicated interactions.

For example:

```text
User
 ↓
Flutter
 ↓
API
 ↓
LLM provider
 ↓
stream
 ↓
UI
```

### State diagram

Very useful when the application contains complicated state machines.

### Dependency graph

Extremely useful for **implementation planning**, not necessarily architecture.

### Task graph

This belongs to implementation planning.

So don't turn diagrams into a checklist like:

> Every project must have 6 diagrams.

Instead:

> Produce the minimum diagrams necessary to make important structure and behavior unambiguous.

---

# 5. Architectural decisions

You also identified an important problem.

`TECHNICAL_DECISIONS.md` shouldn't necessarily exist.

For a simple project, decisions can simply live in:

```text
ARCHITECTURE.md
```

For projects with many meaningful decisions, use ADRs:

```text
docs/adr/
    001-storage.md
    002-state-management.md
    003-provider-abstraction.md
```

An ADR is useful when a decision has:

```text
multiple plausible alternatives
+
meaningful consequences
+
long-term relevance
```

Not:

> We decided to name this class `ConfigParser`.

That's not an ADR.

---

# 6. Define engineering constraints

Now comes the part the earlier workflow badly misplaced.

Create:

```text
QUALITY.md
```

This answers:

> What properties must remain true?

For example:

```text
Architecture invariants
- Presentation layer does not access persistence.

Data invariants
- Imported configurations are normalized before storage.

Reliability requirements
- Malformed external input must not crash the application.

Performance requirements
- Normal config import should complete within X.

Security requirements
- API keys must never enter logs.
```

But there's another document we need.

---

# 7. Define the testing/verification strategy

Only **now** introduce:

```text
TEST_STRATEGY.md
```

You were absolutely right that I previously pulled this document out of nowhere.

Its job is to answer:

> How do we mechanically prove that the important properties in QUALITY.md remain true?

For example:

| Requirement                       | Enforcement               |
| --------------------------------- | ------------------------- |
| UI cannot access DB               | architecture test         |
| Parser removes `<style>`          | unit test                 |
| Config schema is valid            | schema/validation test    |
| API response conforms to contract | integration/contract test |
| Import handles 100k entries       | benchmark                 |
| User can import → edit → export   | E2E test                  |

This relationship is important:

```text
QUALITY.md
     │
     │ says WHAT must remain true
     ↓
TEST_STRATEGY.md
     │
     │ says HOW it is verified
     ↓
tests / linters / benchmarks / CI
```

That's the clean separation.

---

# 8. Build the implementation plan

Now we're ready to ask:

> What do we actually build first?

And yes, **the graph should be first-class**, not just prose buried inside Markdown.

I would therefore have:

```text
IMPLEMENTATION_PLAN.md
IMPLEMENTATION_GRAPH.md
```

or simply a graph embedded in the implementation plan if you want fewer files.

For your style, I'd probably use:

```text
IMPLEMENTATION_PLAN.md
```

containing:

1. task definitions
2. dependency graph
3. execution status

So the graph is not a substitute for the plan; it is one part of it.

For example:

```text
                    ┌───────────────┐
                    │ Project shell │
                    └───────┬───────┘
                            │
                     ┌──────┴──────┐
                     ↓             ↓
              Data model       UI shell
                     │             │
                     ↓             ↓
               Repository      Editor UI
                     │             │
                     └──────┬──────┘
                            ↓
                       Validation
                            │
                            ↓
                     Import / Export
                            │
                            ↓
                       E2E journey
                            │
                            ↓
                          MVP
```

Each node is a task/slice.

The graph tells us:

```text
what depends on what
```

The plan tells us:

```text
what each node actually means
```

---

# 9. The plan is not a todo list

This is an important distinction.

Bad:

```text
[ ] Build frontend
[ ] Build backend
[ ] Add authentication
[ ] Add settings
```

Good:

```text
T1 — Configuration domain model
T2 — Configuration repository
T3 — Config import parser
T4 — Config editor
T5 — Validation pipeline
T6 — Export pipeline
T7 — Import/edit/export E2E journey
```

With dependencies:

```text
T1 → T2 → T3
          ↓
         T5
T1 → T4 → T5
          ↓
         T6
          ↓
         T7
```

Now an agent can reason about what is ready.

---

# 10. Repository agent context

Now we create:

```text
AGENTS.md
```

But its role is much narrower than I previously made it.

It should **not contain the entire engineering methodology**.

It should be the agent's entry point:

```text
Read PRODUCT.md for product requirements.

Read MVP.md for current scope.

Read ARCHITECTURE.md for system architecture.

Read QUALITY.md for invariants.

Read TEST_STRATEGY.md for verification strategy.

Read IMPLEMENTATION_PLAN.md for current work.

Follow repository tooling and verification requirements.

Do not modify architectural constraints without updating
the authoritative architectural documentation.
```

That's enough.

Then domain-specific agent guidance can live near the domain:

```text
src/yomitan/AGENTS.md
src/parser/AGENTS.md
```

when actually necessary.

---

# PART II — The real development loop

Everything above establishes the project.

Now we enter the **repeating loop**.

This is the core of the whole system.

```text
SELECT READY TASK
        ↓
UNDERSTAND TASK
        ↓
DEFINE SMALL CONTRACT
        ↓
WRITE TEST / CHECK
        ↓
IMPLEMENT
        ↓
LOCAL VERIFICATION
        ↓
DEBUG IF NECESSARY
        ↓
UPDATE STATE
        ↓
COMMIT
        ↓
CI
        ↓
MERGE
        ↓
NEXT READY TASK
```

This repeats dozens or hundreds of times.

That is what was missing from the previous version.

---

# 11. Select the next task

The agent does **not** receive:

> "Build the MVP."

It receives:

> "Work on the next ready task."

The agent reads:

```text
IMPLEMENTATION_PLAN.md
```

and finds a task whose dependencies are complete.

For example:

```text
T3 — Config parser

Status: READY

Dependencies:
T1 complete
T2 complete
```

It becomes the current task.

You don't need to paste the entire development methodology into the prompt every time because the repository itself contains it.

---

# 12. Define the small contract

This is where the video's "small validated bricks" actually enters the workflow.

The agent examines the task and identifies the smallest meaningful contract.

Example:

Task:

> Implement Yomitan definition parser.

Instead of immediately writing the full parser:

```text
HTML
 ↓
parser
 ↓
definition
```

the agent identifies contracts such as:

```text
C1: locate definition node
C2: remove irrelevant nodes
C3: preserve required markup
C4: extract definition text
C5: return normalized representation
```

Now we get:

```text
task
 ↓
small contracts
 ↓
tests
 ↓
implementation
```

That is the practical meaning of a "validated brick."

---

# 13. Write the test for the brick

The agent creates the smallest appropriate test.

Example:

```text
Input:
<div>
  definition
  <style>huge CSS...</style>
</div>

Expected:
definition
```

The test becomes the executable contract.

The important point is:

**this happens before or alongside implementation of the brick, not after the entire feature is completed.**

---

# 14. Implement the brick

The agent now implements only enough code to satisfy the contract.

Then:

```text
small test
    ↓
implementation
    ↓
small test passes
```

Then the next brick:

```text
C1
 ↓
C2
 ↓
C3
 ↓
C4
```

You are progressively constructing the feature from validated components.

---

# 15. What happens to `./verify`?

Now its place is clear.

There are **two levels of verification**.

### Local/inner verification

During implementation:

```text
targeted test
```

answers:

> Does this brick work?

Then increasingly broad checks:

```text
unit tests
integration tests
etc.
```

answer:

> Did my change break related things?

### `./verify`

`./verify` means:

> Run the repository's complete mandatory local quality gate.

Example:

```text
./verify
 ├── formatting
 ├── lint
 ├── static/type analysis
 ├── unit tests
 ├── integration tests
 ├── architecture checks
 └── other mandatory checks
```

An agent should normally run targeted checks while iterating and then `./verify` before declaring the task complete.

That makes the hierarchy:

```text
tiny test
    ↓
targeted test suite
    ↓
./verify
    ↓
CI
```

---

# 16. Where regression tests fit

Now regression testing has a proper location.

Suppose the parser bug escapes:

```text
<style> leaked into result
```

The agent fixes it.

Then the development loop includes:

```text
BUG
 ↓
diagnose
 ↓
fix
 ↓
add regression test
 ↓
verify
 ↓
commit
```

The regression test becomes part of the permanent repository test suite.

So regression tests are **not a separate phase**.

They're a rule inside the debugging branch of the development loop:

```text
normal development
       ↓
bug discovered
       ↓
fix bug
       ↓
add regression test
       ↓
continue normal development
```

---

# 17. Completion of a task

Once implementation and verification are successful, the agent updates state.

For example:

```text
IMPLEMENTATION_PLAN.md

T3 — Config parser
Status: COMPLETE
```

The agent may also update technical documentation **only if something actually changed**.

For example:

```text
architecture changed
→ update ARCHITECTURE.md

new invariant introduced
→ update QUALITY.md

new contract introduced
→ update relevant contract/spec

bug fixed
→ add regression test
```

No pointless documentation churn.

---

# 18. Git happens here

Git is therefore not "step 13."

It is part of the development loop.

```text
task
 ↓
implementation
 ↓
tests
 ↓
./verify
 ↓
commit
 ↓
push
 ↓
CI
 ↓
merge
```

For your solo workflow:

```text
main
│
├── feature/task-123
│
└── feature/task-124
```

Agents work on task branches.

`main` is protected and should remain healthy.

---

# 19. What should agents do with Git?

I'd give agents permission to:

```text
create branch
commit
push branch
```

but **not automatically merge to main**.

The merge can be your lightweight human control point.

For multiple agents, the rule becomes:

```text
Agent A → branch A
Agent B → branch B
Agent C → branch C
```

Never let five agents independently modify the same working tree.

A shared branch/worktree is an unnecessary source of collisions.

---

# 20. CI

After the agent pushes:

```text
GitHub
 ↓
CI
 ↓
./verify
 ↓
pass/fail
```

This is the second independent verification layer.

Local verification answers:

> Did the agent's environment pass?

CI answers:

> Does the repository pass in a clean environment?

This catches things such as:

```text
forgotten file
undeclared dependency
platform mismatch
environment-specific issue
generated files missing
```

---

# PART III — MVP completion

Eventually the graph reaches:

```text
all MVP tasks complete
```

But you're not done merely because all checkboxes are green.

Now run an MVP validation.

---

# 21. MVP validation

This is a specific checkpoint.

Ask:

```text
Can a real user complete the core journey defined in MVP.md?
```

This is where E2E testing becomes particularly valuable.

For example:

```text
Launch
 ↓
Import config
 ↓
Edit config
 ↓
Validate
 ↓
Export
 ↓
Open exported config
```

The E2E test proves the **system**, not individual components.

Now you have:

```text
unit/brick tests
+
integration tests
+
architecture checks
+
E2E
+
./verify
```

and the MVP acceptance criteria.

---

# 22. Release

Now:

```text
MVP
 ↓
release candidate
 ↓
CI
 ↓
version/tag
 ↓
release
```

You might create:

```text
CHANGELOG.md
```

and release notes.

For a personal app, don't turn this into enterprise release management.

---

# PART IV — After MVP

This is where your earlier workflow jumped awkwardly.

After the MVP, you enter an **evolution loop**.

```text
new feature idea
       ↓
product decision
       ↓
update PRODUCT.md if necessary
       ↓
update MVP/roadmap scope
       ↓
architecture impact?
       ↓
update architecture if necessary
       ↓
add implementation tasks
       ↓
development loop
       ↓
release
```

This repeats indefinitely.

---

# 23. Architecture is not frozen

This is crucial.

You don't do:

```text
architecture
 ↓
freeze forever
```

Instead:

```text
architecture
 ↓
implementation
 ↓
real-world knowledge
 ↓
new requirement
 ↓
architecture reassessment
```

When a new feature doesn't fit:

### Small change

Implement it normally.

### Moderate architectural change

Update `ARCHITECTURE.md`, possibly an ADR, then create tasks.

### Major change

Stop normal feature development and create an explicit architecture-change effort.

For example:

```text
Flutter state architecture
        ↓
new requirement exposes limitations
        ↓
architecture investigation
        ↓
decision
        ↓
migration plan
        ↓
migration tasks
        ↓
normal development resumes
```

---

# The complete repository

I'd therefore simplify your proposed repository to:

```text
PRODUCT.md
MVP.md
ARCHITECTURE.md
QUALITY.md
TEST_STRATEGY.md
IMPLEMENTATION_PLAN.md
AGENTS.md

src/
tests/

docs/
    adr/              # only if useful
    architecture/    # only if useful
    domain/           # only if useful

.github/
    workflows/

verify
```

And potentially:

```text
DATA_MODEL.md
API_CONTRACTS.md
STATE_MODEL.md
PERFORMANCE.md
SECURITY.md
```

**only when justified by the project.**

That gives the senior architecture model freedom without turning documentation into chaos.

---

# The authority hierarchy

This is something I would explicitly put into `AGENTS.md`.

```text
PRODUCT.md
    ↓
Defines product intent and requirements

MVP.md
    ↓
Defines current scope

ARCHITECTURE.md
    ↓
Defines technical structure

Specialized technical docs
    ↓
Define detailed contracts/models for their domain

QUALITY.md
    ↓
Defines properties/invariants that must remain true

TEST_STRATEGY.md
    ↓
Defines how those properties are mechanically verified

IMPLEMENTATION_PLAN.md
    ↓
Defines current implementation work and dependencies

AGENTS.md
    ↓
Explains how an agent navigates and operates within all of this
```

And critically:

```text
CODE ≠ specification
```

Code implements the specifications.

Tests enforce important properties of the specifications.

---

# The agent's actual operating loop

This is what I think you were ultimately trying to get to.

An agent receives essentially:

> **Implement the next ready task.**

That's it.

The repository tells it the rest.

The agent should then autonomously do:

```text
1. Read AGENTS.md
2. Read relevant product/MVP requirements
3. Read relevant architecture
4. Read relevant quality rules
5. Inspect existing code
6. Select current task
7. Identify its smallest contracts
8. Add/update appropriate tests
9. Implement
10. Run targeted checks
11. Run ./verify
12. Fix failures
13. Add regression tests when bugs were discovered
14. Update implementation state
15. Update technical docs only when necessary
16. Commit
17. Push
18. CI verifies
19. Task becomes complete
20. Select next ready task
```

And **that** is the autonomous agentic development system.

---

# Failure handling lives inside this loop

Don't create another numbered phase.

The development loop branches:

```text
                  TASK
                    │
              implement
                    │
                verify
                    │
           ┌────────┴────────┐
           │                 │
         PASS              FAIL
           │                 │
        finish          diagnose
                             │
                 ┌───────────┼───────────┐
                 ↓           ↓           ↓
             code bug   missing test   bad design/spec
                 │           │           │
                 └───────────┴───────────┘
                             ↓
                         fix cause
                             ↓
                       verify again
```

After repeated failure, escalate.

I'd use something like:

```text
Attempt 1–2:
agent investigates and fixes

Attempt 3:
agent must stop blind retrying and diagnose root cause

If unclear:
stronger reasoning/debugging model

If still unclear:
human decision
```

And when the root cause is identified:

```text
missing specification
→ improve specification

missing invariant
→ add QUALITY rule

missing protection
→ add test/linter/check

architecture problem
→ update architecture + migration/task plan

tooling problem
→ improve tooling
```

That's how the system gets stronger over time.

---

# What I would change from your current version

The biggest conceptual changes are:

### Replace this:

```text
Product
↓
Architecture
↓
Plan
↓
Build
↓
Testing
↓
Git
↓
Release
```

with:

```text
INITIALIZE PROJECT

Product
↓
MVP
↓
Architecture
↓
Quality/invariants
↓
Verification strategy
↓
Implementation graph
↓
Agent context
↓
──────────────────────────
      DEVELOPMENT LOOP
──────────────────────────
select task
↓
small contract
↓
test/check
↓
implement
↓
targeted verification
↓
./verify
↓
state/docs
↓
commit
↓
CI
↓
merge
↓
next task
──────────────────────────

MVP validation
↓
release

then repeatedly:

FEATURE / CHANGE
↓
product decision
↓
architecture reassessment
↓
implementation graph
↓
DEVELOPMENT LOOP
↓
release
```

That is the version I'd recommend you actually adopt.

The **most important thing** is that the "agentic" part isn't a clever prompt. It's the repository becoming a **self-describing, mechanically enforced development environment** where:

```text
requirements
→ architecture
→ constraints
→ tests
→ implementation tasks
→ automated verification
→ git/CI
```

form one connected system.

That also resolves your concern about "what do I tell the agent?" You don't want to repeatedly inject a giant methodology prompt. You want the agent to enter a repository whose structure already encodes the methodology.

I can turn this revised structure into a clean one-page/printable workflow document next.

9: - **Target Environments:**
10:   - **Desktop:** Arch Linux (Anki Desktop Qt6 WebEngine, widescreen, content-driven dense layout).
11:   - **Mobile:** Samsung Galaxy A50 (AnkiDroid / WebKit, small screen, ultra-compact zero-waste vertical spacing).
12: - **Core Files:**
13:   - `Card 1 - Front.template.anki` (Front card HTML & dynamic scaling script + Mature Word Mode: interval-gated word-only front, threshold const `LONG_INTERVAL_DAYS`, AnkiConnect `guiCurrentCard`/content-search + AnkiDroid JS API retrieval, graceful sentence fallback)
14:   - `Card 1 - Back.template.anki` (Back card HTML, circular audio & lightbox script)
15:   - `Card 1 - Style.css` (Tokyo Night & Aki Paper themes, responsive clamps)
16:   - `fetch_anki_fields.py` (Read-only AnkiConnect dump of the live note-type field names into gitignored `.anki_fields.json`; stdlib only — see rule 0)
17:   - `finish.sh` (⭐ THE post-change routine: tests + sync + export + commit + push + release in one command; flags: `--local`, `--minor`, `--prompt`)
18:   - `sync_to_anki.py` (Pushes templates & styles to Anki via Anki-Connect; snapshots the live Anki state into gitignored `backups/<timestamp>/` before overwriting; step 1 of finish.sh)
19:   - `release_apkg.py` (Exports sample deck to `dist/anki-japanese-template.apkg` via Anki-Connect `exportPackage`; step 2 of finish.sh)
20:   - `tests/` (Regression tests for the Definition Compactor CSS; step 0 of finish.sh, auto-skipped while absent)
21:   - `chat_history/` (Archived user prompts from Gemini CLI & Opencode)
22: 
23: ## 📦 Sample Deck & Release Facts
24: - Release automation exports the deck **"My Life Decks::Japanese::anki-japanese-template"** via the Anki-Connect `exportPackage` action (the only verified working export action).
25: - The apkg is **gitignored** (`*.apkg`) — it is distributed exclusively as a **GitHub Release asset** named `anki-japanese-template.apkg`, never committed to the repo.
26: - Releases are git tags (`v<major>.<minor>.<patch>`); `finish.sh` auto-bumps the patch segment (`--minor` bumps the minor segment instead, for multi-feature releases).
27: 
28: ---
29: 
30: ## 🔒 Mandatory Golden Rules for All Agents
31: 
32: ### 0. Field-Name Bootstrap (fields live in Anki, never in the repo)
33: - Note-type fields are managed **exclusively inside the Anki UI**. The repo keeps **no** static field list (the old `JapNoteType.json` snapshot was deleted for exactly this reason — it went stale).
34: - At session start, **before** any template work, run `python3 fetch_anki_fields.py` and read the generated `.anki_fields.json` snapshot for exact field names and descriptions.
35: - If Anki / Anki-Connect is unreachable, **stop and ask the user to start Anki** — never guess, invent, or reuse field names from memory or chat history.
36: 
37: ### 1. Local Files are the Single Source of Truth
38: - **Never** instruct the user to edit HTML/CSS inside the Anki application UI.
39: - All edits must happen directly in the local `.template.anki` and `.css` files.
40: 
41: ### 2. THE Release Workflow (one command, not five)
42: > **⚠ LLM failure-mode warning:** routine multi-step endings get forgotten late in a session (it already happened once). Never run the steps manually — that is how steps get dropped.
43: 
44: After **every** modification to the note templates/CSS, finish with exactly one command:
45: 
46: ```bash
47: ./finish.sh "<semantic commit message>"
48: ```
49: 
50: It runs, in order, and stops on first failure:
51: 0. `tests/` — regression suites: `test_compactor.py` (Definition Compactor CSS) + `test_templates.py` (structural invariants: front-furigana ban, aria-labels, restart-only audio, lightbox close semantics, balanced conditionals, a11y CSS). Auto-skipped while `tests/` is absent.
52: 1. `sync_to_anki.py` — push Front/Back/CSS into the live Anki profile; snapshots the live Anki state into gitignored `backups/<timestamp>/` first
53: 2. `release_apkg.py` — export the sample deck to `dist/anki-japanese-template.apkg`
54: 3. `git add -A && git commit` — snapshot (includes the chat_history log)
55: 4. `git push origin main`
56: 5. `gh release create` — auto-bump patch tag and upload the apkg asset
57: 
58: **Flags:**
59: - `--local` — steps 0–3 only (sync + export + commit, no push/release). Use while iterating a multi-part change; finish the batch with a full run (no flag).
60: - `--minor` — bump the minor version segment (v1.x.0) instead of patch, for multi-feature releases.
61: - `--prompt "<user prompt>"` — archives the prompt to `chat_history/opencode_prompts.txt` before anything runs (AGENTS.md rule 3 encoded in the script).
62: 
63: Do not skip, reorder, or substitute steps. The user reviews cards inside Anki after this command.
64: 
65: ### 3. Prompt Archiving
66: - Append every new user prompt to `chat_history/opencode_prompts.txt` **before** running `./finish.sh` (so the log is included in the same commit).
67: - Format: prompt text, then `---` separator on its own line.
68: - Preferred: let the script do it via `./finish.sh --prompt "<user prompt>" "<message>"` — zero chance of forgetting.
69: 
70: ### 4. Technical Constraints
71: - **Zero-Reflow Furigana:** Furigana must remain hidden by default and reveal on `:hover` (Desktop) / `:active` (Mobile) without shifting surrounding Japanese text by even a single pixel (uses absolute ruby positioning).
72: - **Audio Buttons:** Uses custom standalone circular SVG progress buttons (`文` for sentence, `言葉` for word). Avoid default browser audio controls.
73: - **Mature Word Mode (interval-gated front):** When the current card's SRS interval ≥ `LONG_INTERVAL_DAYS` (const in the Front template script, default 365), the front shows only the `Expression` instead of the sentence (anti-overlearning). Hard rules: no `{{Interval}}` marker exists — the interval must be fetched at render time (AnkiConnect `guiCurrentCard` during active review, `findCards` content search in the Browse previewer, AnkiDroid JS API `ankiGetCardInterval()` on mobile); NEVER fetch AnkiConnect (`127.0.0.1:8765`) from a mobile WebView (Android/iOS UA) — the refused connection surfaces natively as `Failed to load 'downloadfile.bin'`; any retrieval failure MUST degrade to the normal sentence front; no Python/addon/new-field solutions; listening-mode fronts stay untouched; keep the anti-flash `visibility:hidden` gate.
74: - **Screen Real Estate:** Screen space is precious. Avoid adding useless debug badges or verbose header labels (`Sentence`, `Listening Card Active`, etc.).
75: - **Minimal JS Footprint:** Avoid external libraries. All scripts must be vanilla, scoped, and resilient to Anki WebView DOM re-use.

(End of file - total 75 lines)
</content>
```

313:         const parseDroidInterval = raw => {
314:           let value = raw;

316:           for (let depth = 0; depth < 6; depth++) {
317:             if (typeof value === 'number') {
318:               if (!Number.isFinite(value) || value < 0) {
319:                 throw new Error('No usable card interval');
320:               }

322:             }
323: 
324:             if (typeof value === 'string') {
325:               const text = value.trim();
326: 
327:               if (!text) {
328:                 throw new Error('Empty interval response');
329:               }
330: 
331:               // Ordinary numeric string.
332:               const number = Number(text);
333:               if (Number.isFinite(number)) {
334:                 value = number;
335:                 continue;
336:               }
337: 
338:               // JSON-encoded response.
339:               try {
340:                 value = JSON.parse(text);
341:               } catch (_) {
342:                 throw new Error(
343:                   'Unrecognized interval response: ' + text.slice(0, 120)
344:                 );
345:               }
346:               continue;
347:             }
348: 
349:             if (value && typeof value === 'object') {
350:               if (
351:                 value.success === false ||
352:                 value.success === 'false'
353:               ) {
354:                 throw new Error('AnkiDroid reported success=false');
355:               }
356: 
357:               if (
358:                 Object.prototype.hasOwnProperty.call(value, 'value')
359:               ) {
360:                 value = value.value;
361:                 continue;
362:               }
363: 
364:               if (
365:                 Object.prototype.hasOwnProperty.call(value, 'interval')
366:               ) {
367:                 value = value.interval;
368:                 continue;
369:               }
370:             }
371: 
372:             throw new Error('Unsupported interval response');
373:           }
374: 
375:           throw new Error('Unexpectedly nested interval response');
376:         };
377: 
378:         const getAnkiDroidInterval = async () => {
379:           try {

383:             };
384: 
385:             let api;
386:             let apiKind;
387: 
388:             if (typeof AnkiDroidJS === 'function') {
389:               // Constructor-based API.
390:               api = new AnkiDroidJS(options);
391:               apiKind = 'constructor';
392:             } else {
393:               // Direct/native bridge API.
394:               api = AnkiDroidJS;
395:               apiKind = 'direct';
396: 
397:               if (typeof api.init === 'function') {
398:                 const initialized = await withBridgeTimeout(
399:                   api.init(JSON.stringify(options)),
400:                   1000
401:                 );
402: 
403:                 if (
404:                   initialized === false ||
405:                   initialized === 'false'
406:                 ) {
407:                   throw new Error('AnkiDroid API initialization failed');

410:             }

412:             if (
413:               !api ||

415:             ) {
416:               throw new Error('ankiGetCardInterval is unavailable');
417:             }
418: 
419:             /* New-reviewer stub guard: AnkiDroid's fragment-based reviewer
420:                injects a placeholder AnkiDroidJS whose methods only navigate
421:                to 'signal:jsapi' (no data). Never invoke it — the navigation
422:                is unhandled and yields nothing. Detect by method source. */
423:             let methodSrc = '';
424:             try {
425:               methodSrc = Function.prototype.toString.call(api.ankiGetCardInterval);
426:             } catch (_) { /* fall through to the call */ }
427:             if (/signal\s*:\s*jsapi/.test(methodSrc)) {
428:               return {
429:                 interval: null,
430:                 source: 'ankiDroid-stub (reviewer without interval API)'
431:               };
432:             }
433: 
434:             const raw = await withBridgeTimeout(
435:               api.ankiGetCardInterval(),
436:               1000
437:             );
438: 
439:             return {
440:               interval: parseDroidInterval(raw),
441:               source: 'ankiDroid-' + apiKind

443:           } catch (error) {

447:                 (error && error.message ? error.message : String(error))
448:             };
449:           }
450:         };
451:         /* Platform-exclusive retrieval. Phones use ONLY the AnkiDroid
452:            bridge (AnkiConnect is unreachable from a mobile WebView — the
453:            refused fetch surfaces natively as "Failed to load
454:            downloadfile.bin"). Desktops use ONLY AnkiConnect (no
455:            AnkiDroidJS global exists there). Neither platform ever
456:            attempts the other's method. */

458:           if (bridgeAvailable()) {

464:                its API around card load, often AFTER card scripts run — so poll
465:                for it while the card is still hidden (no flash risk), then give
466:                up to the sentence front. 700ms cap: the legacy reviewer
467:                injects js-api.js synchronously in <head>, so a longer wait
468:                only delays preview screens, where the sentence fallback is
469:                correct anyway. */
470:             const waitForBridge = async (deadlineMs, stepMs) => {
471:               const start = Date.now();
472:               while (!bridgeAvailable()) {
473:                 if (Date.now() - start >= deadlineMs) return false;
474:                 await new Promise(r => setTimeout(r, stepMs));
475:               }
476:               return true;
477:             };
478:             if (await waitForBridge(700, 100)) {
479:               const r = await getAnkiDroidInterval();
480:               interval = r.interval;
481:               source = r.source;
482:             } else {
483:               /* No bridge script in the page: on AnkiDroid this means a
484:                  preview screen (card browser / template / editor preview),
485:                  never a real review — the legacy reviewer always injects
486:                  js-api.js synchronously via card_template.html <head>.
487:                  Sentence fallback is correct here; review the card for
488:                  real to exercise mature mode. */
489:               source = 'no-bridge-mobile (preview? bridge exists only in reviewer)';
490:             }
491:           }
492:         } else if (!isListening) {
493:           /* DESKTOP-ONLY: AnkiConnect guiCurrentCard -> cardsInfo. The
494:              bridge is never touched here. guiCurrentCard carries no
495:              interval field (per the AnkiConnect action docs), so the
496:              cardsInfo follow-up is mandatory, not redundant. */
497:           try {
498:             const current = await post('guiCurrentCard', {});
499:             if (current && current.cardId) {
500:               const infos = await post('cardsInfo', { cards: [current.cardId] });
501:               if (infos && infos[0]) {
502:                 interval = infos[0].interval;
503:                 source = 'guiCurrentCard';
504:               }
505:             }
506:           } catch (reviewErr) {
507:             console.log('[Mature Word Mode] no active review, trying content search');
508:             const wordEl = document.querySelector('.front-word-display');
509:             const sentEl = document.querySelector('.sentence-display');
510:             const listeningView = document.querySelector('.listening-view');
511:             const NOTE_TYPE = container.dataset.notetype || '';
512:             const exprDom = wordEl ? normText(wordEl.innerText || wordEl.textContent || '') : '';
513:             if (exprDom && !listeningView) {
514:               const query = '"Expression:' + escQuery(exprDom) + '" "note:' + escQuery(NOTE_TYPE) + '"';
515:               const ids = await post('findCards', { query: query });
516:               if (ids && ids.length) {
517:                 const infos = await post('cardsInfo', { cards: ids });
518:                 const fieldText = (info, name) =>
519:                   normText((info.fields && info.fields[name] && info.fields[name].value) || '');
520:                 let matches = infos.filter(i => fieldText(i, 'Expression') === exprDom);
521:                 if (matches.length > 1 && sentEl) {
522:                   const sentDom = normText(sentEl.innerText || sentEl.textContent || '');
523:                   const withSent = matches.filter(i => fieldText(i, 'Sentence') === sentDom);
524:                   if (withSent.length) matches = withSent;
525:                 }
526:                 if (matches.length) {
527:                   interval = matches[0].interval;
528:                   source = 'content-search';
529:                 }
530:               }
531:             }
532:           }
533:         }
534:       } catch (e) {
535:         source = 'error: ' + (e && e.message ? e.message : String(e));
536:         console.warn('[Mature Word Mode]', e);
537:       } finally {
538:         clearTimeout(safetyTimeout);
539:         const elapsedMs = Math.round(nowMs() - t0);
540:         console.log('[Mature Word Mode] source=' + source +
541:           ' ivl=' + String(interval) + ' in ' + elapsedMs + 'ms');
542: 
543:         const LONG_INTERVAL_DAYS = 365;
544: 
545:         // Set to false after confirming that Android works.
546:         const DEBUG_MATURE_MODE = false;
547: 
548:         const listeningView = container.querySelector('.listening-view');
549:         const wordEl = container.querySelector('.front-word-display');
550:         const hasExpression = !!(
551:           wordEl && (wordEl.textContent || '').trim()
552:         );
553: 
554:         wordMode =
555:           typeof interval === 'number' &&
556:           Number.isFinite(interval) &&
557:           interval >= LONG_INTERVAL_DAYS &&
558:           !listeningView &&
559:           hasExpression;
560: 
561:         // Explicitly reset the class as well as enabling it.
562:         wrapper.classList.toggle('word-mode', wordMode);

574:               'overflow-wrap:anywhere;' +

582:             ' | threshold=' + LONG_INTERVAL_DAYS +
583:             ' | source=' + source +

252:     window.openLightbox = function(target) {

266:

272:         document.removeEventListener('keydown', handleKey);

280:       /* Only a click on the backdrop itself (outside the image) closes —
281:          clicks on the enlarged image stop here and never bubble up. */
282:       overlay.onclick = (e) => {

293:     window.currentActiveBtn = window.currentActiveBtn || null;

301:         if (window.currentActiveBtn) {

346:       badges.forEach(badge => {

348:         const match = raw.replace(/,/g, '').match(/\d+(?:\.\d+)?/);
349:         if (!match) return;
350: 
351:         const val = parseFloat(match[0]);
352:         if (isNaN(val) || val <= 0) return;
353: 
354:         // Japanese Frequency Tiers (Top 500 / 2.5k / 7k / 18k / Rare)
355:         let stars = 1;
356:         let tierClass = 'freq-rare';
357:         let tierLabel = 'Rare';
358:         let fillPercent = 15;
359: 
360:         if (val <= 500) {
361:           stars = 5;
362:           tierClass = 'freq-very-common';
363:           tierLabel = 'Very Common (Top 500)';
364:           fillPercent = 95;
365:         } else if (val <= 2500) {
366:           stars = 4;
367:           tierClass = 'freq-common';
368:           tierLabel = 'Common (Top 2.5k)';
369:           fillPercent = 75;
370:         } else if (val <= 7000) {
371:           stars = 3;
372:           tierClass = 'freq-medium';
373:           tierLabel = 'Intermediate (Top 7k)';
374:           fillPercent = 55;
375:         } else if (val <= 18000) {
376:           stars = 2;
377:           tierClass = 'freq-uncommon';
378:           tierLabel = 'Advanced (Top 18k)';
379:           fillPercent = 35;
380:         } else {
381:           stars = 1;
382:           tierClass = 'freq-rare';
383:           tierLabel = 'Rare (18k+)';
384:           fillPercent = 15;
385:         }
386: 
387:         badge.classList.add(tierClass);
388:         /* (per M) in hover/tooltip per instructions; no percentages typed out */
389:             badge.setAttribute('title', `Frequency: ${raw.trim()} • ${tierLabel}`);
390:         badge.setAttribute('aria-label', `Frequency: ${tierLabel}`);
391: 
392:         const fill = badge.querySelector('.frequency-bar-fill');
393:         if (fill) fill.style.width = fillPercent + '%';
394: 
395:         const starContainer = badge.querySelector('.frequency-stars');
396:         if (starContainer) {
397:           starContainer.textContent = '★'.repeat(stars) + '☆'.repeat(5 - stars);
398:         }
399:       });

404:     /* --- DEFINITION TRUNCATOR (3-line cap + click to expand, stays open) ---
405:        .is-truncated is set only when content truly overflows the 3-line
406:        cap, so short glosses never show a misleading fade/chevron. Expand
407:        is one-way: once open it never re-collapses (a second click is a
408:        no-op). Idempotent: safe under Anki WebView DOM re-use. */
409:     window.expandDefinition = function(element) {

425:         box.classList.remove('is-expanded');

428:         box.classList.toggle('is-truncated', overflows);
